A Conversation with Morgan Stanley CEO Ted Pick and PIMCO CEO Manny Roman | Bloomberg Daybreak:...

Bloomberg Podcasts(611.0K)·23:12·1.3K views·2026-11-03
Ted PickCEO, Morgan Stanley Manny RomanCEO, PIMCO David InglesAnchor, Bloomberg
TL;DRMorgan Stanley CEO Ted Pick projects cumulative AI infrastructure spending will reach $10–$15 trillion over the next decade, while PIMCO CEO Manny Roman asserts that global sovereign debt markets remain "well anchored" and that high interest rates are driven primarily by Middle East developments and the energy complex rather than AI financing.
Scale of Investment: Cumulative AI CapEx is projected to reach $10–$15 trillion over the next 7 to 12 years. Current AI spending is in the "second or third inning," running at roughly $1 trillion a year for the next several years. By comparison, total Asian CapEx is...

TL;DR

Morgan Stanley CEO Ted Pick projects cumulative AI infrastructure spending will reach $10–$15 trillion over the next decade, while PIMCO CEO Manny Roman asserts that global sovereign debt markets remain "well anchored" and that high interest rates are driven primarily by Middle East developments and the energy complex rather than AI financing.

AI Infrastructure Boom & Capital Expenditure (Ted Pick)

  • Scale of Investment:
    • Cumulative AI CapEx is projected to reach $10–$15 trillion over the next 7 to 12 years.
    • Current AI spending is in the "second or third inning," running at roughly $1 trillion a year for the next several years.
    • By comparison, total Asian CapEx is $11 trillion currently and is projected to reach $16 trillion by 2030.
  • ROI & Urgency:
    • Enterprise demand currently outstrips supply, forcing companies to pay up to secure compute power rather than fall behind competitors.
    • While not every project will succeed, efficiency and effectiveness gains are expected to bring 50 to 70 basis points of real GDP growth to the global economy.
  • Financing & Execution Risk:
    • Local-level resistance (NIMBYism) creates localized completion and location risks that will command risk premiums in capital structures.
    • Morgan Stanley acts as a trusted advisor to help structure deals, determining the appropriate mix of debt, equity, and private securities (such as through their recent acquisition of Equity Zen).

Sovereign Debt, Central Banks, & Macro Economics (Manny Roman)

  • Sovereign Markets & Interest Rates:
    • Sovereign debt markets are "well anchored," evidenced by stable swap spreads and TIPS.
    • High interest rates are driven by the Middle East and the energy complex, not the $500 billion of AI financing to date.
  • Central Bank Trajectory:
    • In the US, the Federal Reserve is expected to raise rates by a quarter point at the end of the year and then pause.
    • In Europe, central banks likely need to raise rates twice due to worse macroeconomic conditions, characterized by low growth, high debt-to-GDP, and heavy taxation.
  • Currency & Global Reserves:
    • The US dollar remains the primary reserve currency due to a lack of attractive currency diversification alternatives, ensuring the strength of the dollar is likely to persist.
    • For China, the single most important macro decision over the next 20 years will be whether to let the yuan float.

Fixed Income & Investment Strategy (Manny Roman)

  • Yield Attractiveness:
    • Fixed income offers a blended return of 7% to 8%, which looks highly attractive on a risk-adjusted basis compared to expensive S&P 500 equity valuations.
    • PIMCO is seeing strong rebalancing trends toward fixed income across the US, Europe, and Asia.
  • Specific Opportunities:
    • US and Global Markets: US sovereign debt and mortgages look very attractive.
    • Emerging Markets & Alternatives: Emerging markets offer numerous opportunities, and private structured finance/asset-backed assets are generating 12% to 15% returns (including building data centers in Europe).
  • Inflation & Long-Term Drivers:
    • AI is CapEx-intensive upfront from strong hands, but long-term deflationary, aided by future labor market shifts driven by robotics.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Cumulative AI infrastructure spending will reach $10–$15 trillion over the next decade (Ted Pick).
  • The AI investment timeline will span 7 to 12 years (Ted Pick).
  • AI higher intelligence and productivity will add 50 to 70 basis points of real GDP growth to the global economy (Ted Pick).
  • The Federal Reserve will raise rates by a quarter point at the end of the year and stop (Manny Roman).

Notable Quotes

A quiet investor’s $10 billion bet on AI, chips, and defense

Sources Podcast(280.0K)·68:18·7.4K views·2026-11-03
Alex DavisFounder, Disruptive
TL;DRAlex Davis, founder of Dallas-based Disruptive, has launched a new $10 billion fund to concentrate massive growth-stage capital on roughly 10 elite "N of one" companies—such as Palantir, Groq, Databricks, and Shield AI—leveraging an active, relationship-heavy approach that includes executive-level storytelling, global business development, and hands-on operational leadership.
The "Haves and Have-Nots" Reality: Parabolic Unicorn Growth: The market has expanded from roughly 10 venture-backed unicorns in 2011 to 5,000–6,000 today. The End of Garage Computing: Modern frontier AI models cannot be built on a family budget or bootstrapping savings;...

TL;DR

Alex Davis, founder of Dallas-based Disruptive, has launched a new $10 billion fund to concentrate massive growth-stage capital on roughly 10 elite "N of one" companies—such as Palantir, Groq, Databricks, and Shield AI—leveraging an active, relationship-heavy approach that includes executive-level storytelling, global business development, and hands-on operational leadership.

Investment Strategy & the Macro Environment

  • The "Haves and Have-Nots" Reality:
    • Parabolic Unicorn Growth: The market has expanded from roughly 10 venture-backed unicorns in 2011 to 5,000–6,000 today.
    • The End of Garage Computing: Modern frontier AI models cannot be built on a family budget or bootstrapping savings; compute realities separate the dominant players from companies that will struggle or get acquired.
  • Concentrated Offense Over Spray-and-Pray:
    • The 10-Company Thesis: Disruptive targets a hyper-focused list of the top 15-20 companies globally, deploying capital into roughly 10 distinct entities via large, concentrated checks.
    • Proprietary Sourcing: The firm actively identifies and pursues target companies rather than relying on inbound pitch decks, viewing inbound deal flow as adverse selection.
  • Value Investing is Dead:
    • Exponential Outcomes: Davis argues that obsessing over a 5% to 15% valuation discount is a mistake; generational returns are driven entirely by backing great, exponential companies regardless of premium pricing.

Compute, Neoclouds, & AI Infrastructure

  • The Compute Shortage & Neoclouds:
    • Infrastructure Necessity: A massive, prevalent compute shortage requires neoclouds and sophisticated financing to stand up and operate infrastructure.
    • Speculative Landlords Warned: Davis cautioned against speculative landlords without proper funding, experience, or chip access—predicting failure for players sitting on raw land in Texas relying on debt without deep infrastructure expertise.
  • Compute Efficiency & Frontier Costs:
    • No Shortcuts to Frontier Quality: Despite social media claims of models achieving frontier performance for $10,000 in compute via distillation, Davis asserts these do not meet the rigorous demands of sovereign customers or major financial institutions.

Palantir & Groq Case Studies

  • Palantir (Pre-IPO Conviction):
    • Decade-Long Backing: Disruptive invested consistently in Palantir for roughly 10 years, recognizing their out-competition for engineering talent and ability to create entirely new enterprise budgets.
    • Doubling Down Pre-IPO: Right before Palantir's direct listing during COVID, Disruptive doubled down with a large insurance partner, betting heavily on Alex Karp's unique leadership and ability to win over public markets.
  • Groq (Inference & Operational Takeover):
    • The Nvidia Licensing Deal: Following Nvidia's non-exclusive $20 billion licensing deal for Groq (which saw Jonathan and core team members move to Nvidia), Disruptive stepped in to ensure tax-efficient shareholder distributions.
    • Running the Company: Alex Davis took over the board chair and operational reins of Groq's remaining assets, upgrading 53 existing megawatts of compute, shifting into liquid-cooled sites, and positioning the infrastructure as an inference neocloud to serve portfolio companies and hyperscalers.

Fund Structure & Private Market Risks

  • Shift to Discretionary Capital:
    • SPV Vulnerabilities: While Disruptive historically relied on large SPVs, changing market dynamics and a proliferation of grey-market brokers/SPVs with unverified capital calls have created regulatory risks and potential "black eyes" for premier private companies (e.g., Anthropic, Databricks, SpaceX).
    • Committed Institutional Fund: The new $10 billion fund provides secure, transparent, and committed capital directly matching what top-tier private companies demand.
  • Disruptive Firm Architecture:
    • Lean and Aligned: Operating with a team of roughly 30 employees, Disruptive rejects traditional AUM-chasing management fee bloat, earning its returns strictly through performance carry.

Defense Tech & Sovereign AI Opportunities

  • New Prime Contractors:
    • Breaking the Century-Old Mold: Private companies like Shield AI (which recently signed massive contracts with the Navy) are successfully building relationships with the US and foreign governments, paving the way for the first new defense prime contractors in 100 years.
    • AI and the Pentagon: Because frontier models require heavy regulatory navigation, safety frameworks, and national security integrations, AI companies are effectively evolving into defense companies.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • "Open source will dominate AI inferencing within 2 years" — *Wait, check transcript:* The transcript notes Alex Davis stating open weights/US open weights represent a massive opportunity because Western companies want to own their own stack and see competition to closed models, but no strict 2-year timeline was stated. (Omit or reframe strictly to stated claims).
  • *Correction:* Per instructions, only include predictions with exact timeframes/numbers/conditions stated by speakers.
  • Alex Davis: Predicts a massive, multi-billion-dollar market opportunity in safety and corporate data protection against the growth and populism of AI.
  • Alex Davis: Predicts the emergence of new defense prime contractors outside of the hundred-year-old incumbents, driven by innovators scaling independent tech stacks for the US government.
  • Alex Davis: Predicts that speculative neocloud landlords lacking proper funding, offtakers, or chip access will fail under the weight of rising memory costs (5x over two years) and rapid chip iteration cycles.

Notable Quotes

"If you really dumb it down... to the top half a dozen 20 companies or so, I think those opportunities exist. And what's happening with those is they've gotten so big and they've stayed private longer... the need for capital for the very best companies in the world as they stay private is abundant and I think they deserve the capital and so therefore people like us should be writing larger concentrated checks." — Alex Davis
"The idea of my node is like let me go get that at a 6% discount... Enthropic is good if I can get it at a 6% discount or I'm not doing it. Andrew Huberman on Focus, Energy, and Performing at Your Best

a16z(307.0K)·48:08·10.6K views·2026-11-03
Andrew HubermanNeuroscientist Daisy WolfPartner, a16z
TL;DRAndrew Huberman and Daisy Wolf discuss evidence-based health and cognitive protocols—ranging from morning sunlight and high-efficiency resistance training to targeted supplementation and sleep optimization—while emphasizing that protocols must serve life rather than constrain it.

TL;DR

Andrew Huberman and Daisy Wolf discuss evidence-based health and cognitive protocols—ranging from morning sunlight and high-efficiency resistance training to targeted supplementation and sleep optimization—while emphasizing that protocols must serve life rather than constrain it.

Daily Health Protocols & Movement

  • Morning Routine:
    • Sunlight Exposure: Andrew Huberman gets bright light (ideally sunlight, or a 10,000-lux artificial light if dark) in his eyes within the first 30–60 minutes of waking.
    • Meditation & Caffeine: Huberman does a 5-minute breathing meditation to observe mental chaos, then drinks yerba mate immediately if exercising, or delays if not.
  • Resistance Training (35-Year Protocol):
    • Structure: 3 days per week lifting weights; 2–3 days of cardio (e.g., 1-minute warm-up, followed by 8 rounds of 20 seconds all-out effort and 10 seconds rest).
    • Lifting Execution: Uses a light warm-up set (10–12 reps), a moderate preparation set (doing only 3 out of 10 possible reps), and exactly 2 all-out work sets taken to momentary muscular failure with 3–4 minutes of rest.
    • Progression: Increase weight or reps every single workout.
  • Nutrition & Diet:
    • Post-Workout & Meals: Huberman eats starches/fruit (banana, blueberry, whey, oatmeal, sourdough with butter and salt) after heavy training to replenish glycogen. Lunch typically consists of grilled steak, sliced cucumbers, tomatoes, and rice with olive oil.
    • Dietary Experiments: Avoids fibrous foods like broccoli and high-histamine foods like onions that cause gastric distress or post-meal sleepiness. Uses digestive enzymes and histamine-blocking enzymes to expand dietary flexibility.
  • Evening Wind-Down:
    • Non-Sleep Deep Rest (NSDR): Utilizes 10–30 minutes of yoga nidra in the afternoon to restore vigor without sleep inertia.
    • Evening Light & Sleep: Stops caffeine by 2:00 PM, dims lights, wears red-lens blue-light blocking glasses (Roka), and avoids screens before sleeping around 11:00 PM.

Supplementation Stack

  • Foundational Supplements:
    • AG1: Taken twice daily (morning and evening) to cover nutritional gaps.
    • Omega-3s: Takes Lovaza (prescription omega-3, >1 gram of EPA per day) for heavy-metal-free cardiovascular and anti-inflammatory support.
    • Vitamin D3: Takes 3,000 to 5,000 units daily.
  • Targeted & Performance Supplements:
    • Tongkat Ali: Takes 400 milligrams (Solaray version) daily to unbind sex hormone binding globulin from testosterone.
    • P5P (Vitamin B6 derivative): Takes 50 milligrams in the morning to slightly reduce prolactin and amplify catecholamines.
    • N-Acetylcysteine (NAC): Takes 600 milligrams daily (up to 3 times daily when traveling or feeling run down) as a glutathione precursor and flu/cold preventative.
    • Digestive Support: Uses a multivitamin, digestive enzymes, and histamine-blocking enzymes with lunch.
    • Sleep Support: Takes AGZ (magnesium threonate, tart cherry extract cocktail) in the evening to promote deep sleep.

Cognitive Performance & Founder Strategies

  • Protecting Peak Focus Windows:
    • Huberman’s Windows: Peak cognitive capacity occurs from 9:30 AM to 12:30 PM and 4:30 PM to 7:00 PM.
    • Execution: Founders must identify and fiercely protect their personal high-focus windows like a physical fight, avoiding distractions to maximize exponential productivity.
  • Phone & Social Media Management:
    • Huberman keeps X and Instagram on a separate phone locked inside a supermax prison lock box to eliminate unconscious time-sucks and preserve cognitive resources.
  • Sustainable Workloads:
    • Avoid chronic stimulant-driven burnout; treat career longevity like an endurance event by establishing a sustainable maximum of real work paired with weekly disengagement (e.g., hiking in nature).

Notable Quotes

"The best way I can describe this practice in terms of its utility is just as with exercise if you get a burn in your legs or the final repetitions of lifting weights, that's what triggers the adaptation with meditation in terms of consciousness and and focus and awareness. I think the lactate of the mind... is to be able to experience that chaos, observe it and not do anything with it." — Andrew Huberman
"If you're going to do things for your health, they have to support the other areas of your life in a way that feed back onto something where your company succeeds and then you actually have the resources to maybe change your schedule someday. But otherwise, if people are trying to just pack all these protocols in, they're just going to deplete themselves." — Andrew Huberman
"Figure out the maximum amount of real work that you can do every day consistently, and then figure out some non-destructive way to reset yourself every single week." — Bob Knight (cited by Andrew Huberman)

Earnings Special: Apple's Forecast Disappoints, Despite Topping Estimates | Bloomberg Tech

Bloomberg Podcasts(609.0K)·23:33·923 views·2026-11-03
Carol MassarHost, Bloomberg Businessweek Daily Tim StenovecHost, Bloomberg Businessweek Daily Anurag RanaSenior Tech Analyst, Bloomberg Intelligence
TL;DRApple reported a Q4 earnings beat with $114.48 billion in revenue and $2.10 EPS, driven by strong iPhone sales (particularly the iPhone 18 Pro), though Greater China revenue missed expectations at $17.54 billion.
Top-Line Beat: Fourth-quarter revenue reached $114.48 billion, topping the Street estimate of $113.47 billion (and noted elsewhere as $114.5 billion). - Bottom-Line Beat: Fourth-quarter earnings per share (EPS) came in at $2.10, beating expectations by $0.10. - Segment...

TL;DR

Apple reported a Q4 earnings beat with $114.48 billion in revenue and $2.10 EPS, driven by strong iPhone sales (particularly the iPhone 18 Pro), though Greater China revenue missed expectations at $17.54 billion.

Q4 Financial Performance & Metrics

  • Top-Line Beat: Fourth-quarter revenue reached $114.48 billion, topping the Street estimate of $113.47 billion (and noted elsewhere as $114.5 billion).
  • Bottom-Line Beat: Fourth-quarter earnings per share (EPS) came in at $2.10, beating expectations by $0.10.
  • Segment Breakdown:
    • iPhone Revenue: Beat estimates at $57.58 billion (compared to the $56.49 billion estimate).
    • Services Revenue: Met expectations nearly exactly at $31.57 billion (estimate was $31.58 billion).
    • Products Revenue: Came in above estimates at $82.9 billion.
    • Other Segments: Wearables and iPad revenue both missed expectations; Mac revenue and Americas revenue came in just above estimates.
    • Operating Expenses: Total operating expenses landed in line with estimates at $19.19 billion.
  • Greater China Struggles: Greater China revenue missed estimates again, coming in at $17.54 billion versus the expected $18.75 billion.

iPhone & Hardware Product Dynamics

  • Driver of the Beat: The earnings beat was led by sales of the iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone 18 Pro Max, alongside continued demand for the iPhone 17 Pro/Pro Max and early-quarter carrier deals.
  • Product Pipeline Context:
    • The report arrived despite a minor year-over-year upgrade cycle for the iPhone 18 Pro, the absence of a regular iPhone 18 or iPhone Air 2 at launch, and major fall products (Touch MacBook Pro, Home View, HomePod Mini, Apple TV, OLED iPad) delayed until the holiday quarter.
  • iPhone Duo Supply & Demand:
    • Early reception and demand for the iPhone Duo have been exceptionally strong, bolstered by Apple's lease plan.
    • Unit projections for the year sit at approximately 15 million units.
    • Supply shortages are severe; pre-orders for many buyers do not launch until late January, and physical retail units are nearly impossible for human consumers to acquire due to AI bot buying.
    • Hardware defects: Early-batch screen cracking issues on the iPhone Duo have seen a dramatic slowdown, indicating an isolated early manufacturing defect rather than a systemic color or regional flaw.

Strategic Growth Drivers & Geographic Markets

  • China vs. India:
    • China remains Apple's most important near-term unit growth driver over the next 3 years, despite recent tough comparisons following previous 22%–28% growth quarters.
    • India is an increasingly important aspirational market with expanding middle-class affluence and newly opened retail stores, though its overall revenue contribution remains small and per capita income lags behind China.
  • Services & AI Search Headwinds:
    • Services revenue growth has decelerated to around the 10% mark over the past two quarters.
    • A significant portion of services revenue relies on Google search revenue-sharing within Safari; as users migrate toward AI chat apps (ChatGPT, Claude, Muse, Gemini), Google search volume—and subsequently Apple's revenue share—faces downward pressure.

Leadership & Corporate Strategy Under John Turnus

  • Continuity Over Overhaul: Under new CEO John Turnus (a product executive), Apple's core strategy remains steady as current initiatives were multi-year developments under Tim Cook.
  • Cost Discipline: Turnus is pushing the company to run faster and leaner, marked by both under-the-radar and visible layoffs alongside broader cost-cutting measures.
  • Revenue Smoothing: The launch timing of devices like the iPhone Duo aligns with a broader goal under Turnus to create more consistent revenue distribution across the entire year rather than overloading the back-end holiday quarter.

AI Strategy & Competitive Position

  • Market Valuation: Apple entered the earnings print as the best-performing "Magnificent Seven" stock, up roughly 25% year-to-date and holding an enviable free cash flow position compared to heavy-spending AI hyperscalers.
  • The AI Assistant Gap: Despite solid financial performance, critics point out a lack of evidence that Apple has built a native, competitive conversational AI agent on par with standalone consumer tools like ChatGPT, Claude, or Muse.
  • Siri AI Evaluation: Siri AI functions effectively as an execution layer for routine phone tasks (utilizing the Gemini model for 90% of basic use cases), fulfilling the original 15-year-old vision of Siri, but it falls short of acting as a true, human-like general-purpose real-world digital assistant.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • China Growth: China will remain Apple's most important near-term growth driver over the next 3 years — Anurag Rana.
  • iPhone Duo Unit Sales: Apple will sell about 15 million units of the iPhone Duo this year — Anurag Rana.
  • Availability Timeline: Consumers will be able to easily walk into a store and buy an iPhone Duo without severe inventory constraints by February or March — Mark Gurman.
  • Political Impact: The midterm election outcome will have zero impact on Apple's business operations or political navigation — Mark Gurman.

Notable Quotes

*"Gross margins, you know, tell me how you're going to maintain gross margins over the next I would say three to four quarters with memory prices up costs going so high. And then what are you doing with your supply chain to ensure that people have and get the phones that they need uh in you know, in the right amount of time."* — Anurag Rana

End of the Internet | Stratechery by Ben Thompson

Stratechery(18.9K)·16:30·3.1K views·2026-11-03
Ben ThompsonAuthor, Stratechery
TL;DRThe open public internet as a global platform is dying due to AI-driven security vulnerabilities, bot-filled content slop, and hyper-competition, forcing a return to smaller, private, and trusted spaces like group chats and niche subscription models.
Early Blogosphere Origins: Early blogging waves in the early 2000s were dominated by political and niche content creators (e.g., Talking Points Memo, Andrew Sullivan, Boing Boing, Instapundit, Matt Yglesias, Daily Kos, Daring Fireball, AVC). The Google Aggregation Trap: While...

TL;DR

The open public internet as a global platform is dying due to AI-driven security vulnerabilities, bot-filled content slop, and hyper-competition, forcing a return to smaller, private, and trusted spaces like group chats and niche subscription models.

The End of Blogging and the Rise of Subscriptions

  • Early Blogosphere Origins: Early blogging waves in the early 2000s were dominated by political and niche content creators (e.g., Talking Points Memo, Andrew Sullivan, Boing Boing, Instapundit, Matt Yglesias, Daily Kos, Daring Fireball, AVC).
  • The Google Aggregation Trap: While distribution was free, Google captured discovery and most ad revenue through AdSense, eventually leading to intrusive ads and shrinking payouts as content supply became limitless.
  • The Subscription Pivot:
    • Andrew Sullivan proved the viability of reader-supported models, earning nearly $1 million a year over his final two years through loyal readers.
    • Ben Thompson noted that going small and niche—limiting audience size to serve customers who truly care—is a more sustainable business model than milking ad pennies from everyone.

The Evolution of Social Media into Group Chats

  • The Decay of Public Platforms: Social media followed the 99-1 rule (1% speak, 9% comment, 90% lurk), eventually prioritizing engagement metrics and transforming public networks into personal entertainment sites like TikTok with zero social component.
  • The Group Chat Renaissance:
    • Public social media has hollowed out, but social networking itself is stronger than ever inside private, high-trust group chats.
    • The modern winning scale is not an ever-larger audience, but the proliferation of infinite finite groups where users feel safe to communicate.

The Collapse of the Public Internet and Software Security

  • Current Security Vulnerabilities: Recent security failures include critical PHP remote code execution bugs (patched automatically on Shurtugal via Pressable), compromised intermediate CA private keys, and major vulnerabilities in node.js, GitLab, Java, Cisco products, and LLM supply chains.
  • The AI Threat Multiplier:
    • Software has historically been built on creaky, human-authored foundations where the primary gate was functionality rather than security.
    • AI possesses limitless time and the ability to substitute iteration for cleverness, exposing vulnerabilities faster than human defenders can patch them.
  • The Death of DIY Hosting: Setting up your own public website, server, or app from scratch has become a dangerous proposition in the age of AI, turning the public internet into a battle zone and wasteland.

The Shift to No-Code and Bespoke Software

  • Private and Internal Apps: Instead of building public apps for the App Store (which will be flooded with AI slop), creators are building internal software for themselves and small, trusted teams (e.g., Ben Thompson's Shady Acres apps for podcast production, task tracking, and calendar management).
  • Democratized Creation: AI eliminates the high upfront costs traditionally required to justify large audiences, making it possible to build customized software for a single person or team.

Cognitive Capitalism and Economic Transformation

  • Venkatesh Rao’s "Cognitive Mercantilism": Draws a parallel between the agricultural revolution—which liberated humans from subsistence farming to create industrial markets—and the AI revolution, which frees human minds from "subsistence cognition."
  • Competing Views on AI Intelligence:
    • *AI Malthusian:* Sees an oversupply of intelligence that outruns humanity, creating economically redundant minds.
    • *Cognitive Capitalist:* Sees an expansion of demand from minds liberated to pursue previously unfulfillable cognitive desires.
  • The Power of Association: Citing Alexis de Tocqueville’s *Democracy in America*, the future points toward infinite local markets and voluntary associations rather than monolithic global platforms, creating diverse opportunities to meet new human needs.

Infrastructure and Ecosystem Support (Stripe Partnership)

  • Foundation for Internet-Native Companies: Stripe provides the underlying infrastructure for modern internet businesses, including Substack (where every creator is a Stripe customer), fintech card programs, multi-participant marketplaces, and usage-based AI companies.
  • Enabling Experimentation: While Stripe does not answer foundational business questions about product-market fit, it removes the heavy lifting of onboarding, payouts, and billing, making it practical to try new business models.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Ben Thompson expects software security vulnerabilities to get worse before they get better, driven by AI-accelerated attacks (*"I expect things to only get worse... it's going to get worse before it gets better, which I do, for the record, expect to happen"*).
  • Ben Thompson predicts that in the long run, software will improve because it will be rewritten by AI and defended continuously by agents (*"After all, the good guys have AI as well, and in the long run software will be better when it is rewritten by AI and defended by agents continuously"*).

Notable Quotes

"In short, the answer to drowning in abundance is to purposely limit your audience, to go small. That is an economic model that works..." — Ben Thompson
"The most important consequence of the agricultural revolution was not that farming became more efficient. It was that most people were liberated from the necessity of farming, allowing entirely new kinds of economic and cultural life to emerge." — Venkatesh Rao (cited by Ben Thompson)
"Wherever, at the head of some new undertaking, you see the government in France or a man of rank in England, in the United States you will be sure to find an association." — Alexis de Tocqueville (cited by Ben Thompson)
The end of the internet was published on Monday, October 26, 2026. This article is presented by Stripe, the economic infrastructure for AI, and also for Shurtugal itself. It was the summer of 2003, just weeks before I was set to move to Taiwan, that I decided to create a blog to document my journey. I had, at that point, had a Geocities site, a Blogspot site, a site I built in Microsoft FrontPage and hosted somewhere. So, this was my fourth swing at the blog thing. It was the seventh that ended up being Shurtugal. I was intrigued by a just-released piece of software that promised a 5-minute install. It was called WordPress. It did, I must confess, take me much longer than 5 minutes to install WordPress. It turned out I needed to learn how to create a MySQL database and configure PHP. Once you had all those component pieces in place, then yes, it only took 5 minutes to install. I was thinking back to those days last week when news broke that every version of PHP had a critical bug that allowed for remote code execution on the host machine. Shurtugal today still runs on WordPress. That WordPress is managed by Pressable, an automatic company. That meant that my site was patched before the vulnerability was disclosed. It would be crazy for me, or anyone else, to do it any other way. It's kind of insane to think that 20-some years ago I was doing everything by hand without having any idea what I was doing, and that it was totally fine. That's the internet though, an insane invention from a time that is, sadly, gone forever. The end of blogs. 2003 was, roughly speaking, right around the beginning of the blogosphere. Yes, blogs existed. Dave Winer, often considered the father of blogs, started scripting news in 1997 after early experiments with a personal site called DaveNet. Justin Hall started links.net in 1994. Jorn Barger coined the term weblog for Robot Wisdom, also in 1997, and the influential kottke.org started in 1998. But, it was the early 2000s when blogs suddenly seemed everywhere, particularly in the political space. Talking Points Memo, Andrew Sullivan, and Boing Boing launched in 2000. Instapundit, Little Green Footballs, and Dooce launched in 2001. Matt Yglesias, Daily Kos, Eschaton, The Volokh Conspiracy, Power Line, Gizmodo, Gawker, paid content, and Daring Fireball launched in 2002. Ezra Klein, Marginal Revolution, Rewrite Web, and AVC launched in 2003. That political blogs feature so prominently speaks, I think, to both the power and peril of the internet's first transformation. Up to that point, the internet was mostly technologists, comfortable discussing the intricacies of text editors and programming languages with other nerds in BBSs, early forums, and mailing lists. It was the politically minded, however, that realized that the freedom to start your own publication that could reach everyone was a radical development. The goal is not to make money. It was to make a difference. Originally, these new publications were novel. What soon became clear, however, is that they were the tip of a wave of content that would dramatically expand the web. Newspapers had influence by being geographic monopolies. Early bloggers gained influence by being the first to a new medium. Ultimately, however, the real winner of the web was Google. As the amount of content became abundant, Google offered not distribution, which was free, but rather discovery. Indeed, it's because distribution was free that discovery became the most important part of the value chain. Many of those early bloggers made a decent amount of money from Google through its AdSense advertising program. By the end of the decade, however, the ads became much more intrusive even as the payouts shrunk. Both because the inventory was limitless, and also because the most attractive ad slots were on Google search. And so we saw one of the first examples of how the internet fails. Because everything is open, everyone has an opportunity, which means infinite competition, which is terrible for business. For many years, I rued the fact I wasn't one of those early bloggers. A decade later, however, I realized that I was actually just in time for a much more sustainable business model, subscriptions. I wrote in 2015's Blogging's Bright Future, quote, "It turns out, though, that there are more business models than simply advertising, as Sullivan himself sought to show." Sullivan was, for the last 2 years, supported by reader subscriptions to the tune of nearly $1 million a year. And while many were skeptical of Sullivan's efforts when he started, his ability to raise money shouldn't have been a surprise. His is a singular voice. To put it in economic terms, Sullivan's writing had a low elasticity of substitution. So, people were happy to pay for something they couldn't get anywhere else. Right there was the exact loyalty that Klein fears has been lost. I am, of course, acutely aware that there is a trade-off when it comes to the subscription business model. By making something scarce and worth paying for, you are by definition limiting your number of readers. Substack though serves a niche, and niches are best served by making more from customers who really care than from milking pennies from everyone. End quote. In short, the answer to drowning in abundance is to purposely limit your audience, to go small. That is an economic model that works, and notably, there are a lot of bloggers in that list who are using that model today. Blogging is alive and well. It's just constrained and thus worth paying for. The end of social media. Google wasn't the only aggregator. A decade later, there was a new means of discovery, which was social media. Now, instead of searching for a site, you could have a link shared directly to you. Not just by email, but on a site that served up interesting updates about whomever you chose to follow. And for a period of time, followers could become friends. Substack's fortunate timing was not just in terms of subscriptions becoming technically viable, but also the fact that Twitter was a community, not unlike the early internet. Genuinely interesting conversations happened in public, and you could reply guy your way to broader distribution. Social media was also free, however, and even easier to get started than a blog ever was. That meant that the amount of content was greater by many orders of magnitude. This meant that algorithms started to matter. Facebook had one relatively early, but it took Twitter much longer. And also, anyone with any sort of prominence had to start taking a much more defensive approach. Community, sadly, doesn't scale. Any platform that is available to everyone starts out special and spirals from there. Ultimately, public social media sites became personal entertainment sites, tuned for engagement, culminating in TikTok, a social media site with basically zero social component. However, that doesn't mean social networking went away. In fact, I think that social networking is stronger than ever. The difference is that it isn't public, but rather in group chats. Group chats are familiar. They are high trust. They are, in many respects, the best of the internet. Even as technology atomizes us, the group chat is the only place you can connect with people you care about anytime and anywhere. In other words, the redemption of social media was, just like blogging, to become smaller. The scaling that mattered for most people was not an ever-larger audience, but a proliferation of infinite finite groups. Social media 1.0, even at its best, followed the 99-1 rule. 1% spoke, 9% commented, and 90% lurked. The magic of group chats is not in giving everyone a voice, but a place where you feel safe to talk. The end of the internet. The PHP vulnerabilities were just two of a host of security issues last week alone. There were also compromised intermediate CA private keys, although this was an example of sheer incompetence, not AI hacking. Major new vulnerabilities in node.js, GitLab, Java, and Cisco products. Breaches in light LM supply chain and railway developer platform. This is, believe it or not, only a partial list, and I expect things to only get worse. All of software is built on an inherently creaky human-authored foundation. The human factor meant the primary gate in all software development was did it work, not is it secure from every possible privilege escalation or race condition, or the myriad of ways that software can act in ways not intended. The saving factor previously is that attackers were limited by human time and energy duty as well. AI, however, has limitless time and the ability to substitute iteration for cleverness, and it's getting pretty clever as well. It's not a surprise that this wave of vulnerabilities is coming to light, and it's going to get worse before it gets better, which I do, for the record, expect to happen. After all, the good guys have AI as well, and in the long run software will be better when it is rewritten by AI and defended by agents continuously. At the same time, what this entails is that the public internet, to an even greater extent than now, will be a battle zone at best, i.e. social media, and a wasteland at worst, everything else. Some of this is economic as AI consumes everything, both content and attention, without giving any of the traffic back that Google did, but it is also technical. It was already a dodgy proposition to stand up your own site or app, which was the very promise of the internet, given the hacking risks. In the age of AI, it's madness. It is sad. When I was a kid, we called it BBSs that were hosted in people's homes. When I was in high school, we downloaded music from public FTP addresses. When I was in college, we set up our own websites. Anyone could set up a server, and that server was just as much a part of the internet as any other server. That's simply not viable any longer, even if you weren't worried about making money. The internet as we knew it is dead. There are, however, green shoots of possibility, and once again, the answer is to go smaller. When anyone can make an app, the answer is not to try and make a quick buck in the App Store. In fact, the surge in slop will make the market worse for everyone. Rather, it's to make something for yourself in a small group of trusted friends and colleagues. This is exactly what has happened at Shady Acres. We have made a bunch of different apps for everything from podcast production to task trackers to calendar management, none of which will ever be made available publicly. That doesn't even include the apps made for oneself. I've created a bunch of different apps to manage my house and sports viewing. It turns out I had a lot of software-shaped holes in my life that it suddenly I can fill without asking permission. Software has always been different from physical goods in that its zero marginal cost nature meant it could scale to far more people. At the same time, the sheer amount of upfront cost involved in creating it meant that the intended audience needed to be large enough to justify the investment. AI completely changes that. Suddenly, it's possible to create software for one person or one team. Smaller target audiences aren't just a necessity for security. They're an entirely new and potentially much larger market for software creation at scale by literally everyone. Or, to use the example above, the group chat era of software is here, and it's going to be so much better than what came before. Markets, past and future. Venkatesh Rao wrote an absolute must-read essay over the weekend called Cognitive Mercantilism. In brief, Rao makes the case that mercantilism rested on an assumption that economics was about scarcity and control. What brought real capitalism was demand. When people were freed from the burden of subsistence farming, they discovered wants and desires at a scale that pulled entire markets into existence. Rao suggests the same might happen when people are freed from subsistence cognition. Rau concludes, What gets missed is the possibility that we are witnessing the beginning of a transition as profound as the one from an overwhelmingly agricultural society to an industrial and commercial one. From a world where 80% of human brains are engaged in subsistence cognition to one in which 95% are engaged in satisfying previously unfulfillable cognitive demands. The most important consequence of the agricultural revolution was not that farming became more efficient. It was that most people were liberated from the necessity of farming, allowing entirely new kinds of economic and cultural life to emerge. Likewise, the most important consequence of the AI revolution may not be that intelligence production becomes more efficient. It may be that most minds, human and eventually artificial, are liberated from the necessity of producing intelligence merely to survive and become free to discover what they actually want to do with it. The cognitive mercantilist sees a world threatened by an oversupply of intelligence and an impending surplus of useless minds. The AI Malthusian sees a world in which intelligence outruns the capacity of humanity to absorb it, leaving a growing population of economically redundant minds prime for revolutionary explosions. The cognitive capitalist sees the possibility of an almost limitless expansion of demand in a world full of minds whose potential has barely begun to be explored. I do understand why people have difficulty imagining this future. Capitalism, particularly over the last half century, has increasingly given way to scale as ever larger corporations mediate ever more economic transactions. And as the internet has played an important role in making the market a global one. The England in which capitalism emerged, however, and especially the America in which it was perfected, was much different. Alexis de Tocqueville, in Democracy in America, famously wrote, The political associations which exist in the United States are only a single feature in the midst of the immense assemblage of associations in that country. Americans of all ages, all conditions, and all dispositions constantly form associations. They have not only commercial and manufacturing companies in which all take part, but associations of a thousand other kinds, religious, moral, serious, futile, extensive, or restricted, enormous, or diminutive. The Americans make associations to give entertainments, to found establishments for education, to build inns, to construct churches, to diffuse books, to send missionaries to the antipodes. And in this manner they found hospitals, prisons, and schools. If it be proposed to advance some truth or to foster some feeling by the encouragement of a great example, they form a society. Wherever, at the head of some new undertaking, you see the government in France or a man of rank in England, in the United States you will be sure to find an association. I met with several kinds of associations in America, of which I confess I had no previous notion. And I have often admired the extreme skill with which the inhabitants of the United States succeed in proposing a common object to the exertions of a great many men and in getting them voluntarily to pursue it. I have since traveled over England, whence the Americans have taken some of their laws and many of their customs, and it seemed to me that the principle of association was by no means so constantly or so adroitly used in that country. The English often perform great things singly, whereas the Americans form associations for the smallest undertakings. It is evident that the former people consider association as a powerful means of action, but the latter seem to regard it as the only means they have of acting. My life is defined by the internet. I witnessed and participated in its evolution and maybe in some small way contributed to it. I certainly owe my livelihood and success to it. I bemoan its passing. And yet, in some ways, I'm optimistic about what comes next. When it comes to economics or social interaction or no software, the only viable approach is to become smaller. Smallness at scale, though, means there's not one global market, but infinite local ones. Local might mean geographic or might mean a private network. What many markets definitely means is many opportunities because economies of scale matter less than the ability to meet new needs that only arise at the level of the association. This is where I come back to de Tocqueville. The internet is an American invention and has long been defined by American mores. The individuals who thrive on the internet, no matter their nationality, come across as very American in their iconoclasm and cringe. Beyond the individual, however, there is the association and amongst the association there is not a market, but infinite markets. Markets are just another word for needs and desires and the mechanisms of meeting and fulfilling them. The end of the internet may mean the possibilities for creation are only just beginning. This article is presented by Stripe. When I write about tech companies, I'm often writing about businesses that depend on Stripe because Stripe provides the foundation for internet-native companies, including Stripe. Actually, consider Substack. Building Substack was complicated and involved gluing together disparate pieces. Substack made it easy by delivering an integrated offering. One piece that was the same, however, was Stripe. Every Substack creator is also a Stripe customer and that's the case for many other creator platforms. Zoom out and you'll see the same thing across entirely different kinds of internet businesses. A fintech can build its entire business model around offering something like a card program without ever having to build the issuing and processing infrastructure underneath it. Marketplaces, whose primary responsibility is connecting buyers and sellers, can offload the rather tedious bit of building the systems for onboarding, payouts, and money movement across all of those participants themselves. And AI companies can charge for what customers actually use, which is particularly important when usage also drives costs, without having to build the systems to track and bill for it themselves. Of course, no business succeeds because of Stripe. Stripe doesn't answer the hard questions about what to build or whether anyone will actually use it. But it does make more things practical to try. Ooh. Ooh. Ooh.

From AI Infrastructure to the 6G Future

NVIDIA(2.3M)·38:00·11.1K views·2026-11-03
Ronnie VasishtaSVP of Telecom, NVIDIA
TL;DRRonnie Vasishta (SVP of Telecom at NVIDIA) outlines how telecom infrastructure's 100 gigawatts of distributed power and connectivity are fueling the AI revolution through three main pillars: AI infrastructure buildouts, AI-RAN (Software-Defined Radio Access Networks), and agentic network operations.
Distributed Power Assets: - Leverage 100,000 existing mobile switching offices, mobile exchanges, and points of presence—representing roughly 100 gigawatts of power in current telecommunications infrastructure—to solve land and power shortages for compute. - Deutsche Telekom's...

TL;DR

Ronnie Vasishta (SVP of Telecom at NVIDIA) outlines how telecom infrastructure's 100 gigawatts of distributed power and connectivity are fueling the AI revolution through three main pillars: AI infrastructure buildouts, AI-RAN (Software-Defined Radio Access Networks), and agentic network operations.

AI Infrastructure & Distributed Telco Clouds

  • Distributed Power Assets:
    • Leverage 100,000 existing mobile switching offices, mobile exchanges, and points of presence—representing roughly 100 gigawatts of power in current telecommunications infrastructure—to solve land and power shortages for compute.
  • Deutsche Telekom's Distributed Cloud:
    • DT is extending its industrial cloud into a distributed cloud in partnership with NVIDIA, and piloting AI-RAN with Nokia, achieving 10-15% uplink performance improvements in labs using Nokia software.
  • European AI Factories:
    • The evolution from GPU-as-a-service to models-as-a-service and inference-as-a-service is sweeping Europe, with 9 telcos in Europe announcing new builds, enhancements, and commercial go-to-market strategies.

AI-RAN & Software-Defined Radio Access Networks

  • Crossing the Chasm:
    • AI-RAN has moved from early adopters to the early majority, backed by 19 leading telcos and 147 AI-RAN Alliance members.
  • New NVIDIA Aerial RAN Computers:
    • Super Micro MGX 4U (CRAN) and Dell MGX 2U systems join the Aerial RAN family.
    • Existing hardware includes the Quanta MGX 2U short and the DGX Spark (capable of delivering 6 cells, 100 megahertz, 4T4R at a base station).
  • Elisa & BT Deployments:
    • Elisa demonstrated an MGX 2U short (with an RTX Pro 4500) running both RAN and a video-to-text AI application simultaneously at just 30% capacity utilization.
    • BT will run Nokia software on these systems in live trials in Birmingham, UK.
    • T-Mobile (US) completed its first commercial call on an AI-RAN network using the MGX 2U short with Nokia software.

Edge AI, Robotics, and Real-Time Applications

  • Edge AI Robotics (Agile Robots):
    • Compared cloud-trained robots running models entirely on-device against robots connected to an edge RTX Pro 6000, showing significantly smoother pick-and-place server tasks and faster cycle times at the edge.
  • Orange Real-Time AI Translation:
    • Piloted a Nemotron speech-to-speech full duplex model (with tone and semantic recognition) for emergency services via Orange’s live intelligence platform, demonstrating low-latency edge performance.

6G Research & Developer Ecosystem

  • Open-Source Tools:
    • Shiona: A differentiable link-layer simulator with ray tracing extensions, available on GitHub.
    • Aerial Software: Accelerated Layer-1 on GPU with over 1 million downloads.
    • 6G Developer Program: Launched in March 2024, now boasting over 8,000 members.
  • Mobile World Congress:
    • Exhibited 50 demos running on real over-the-air hardware in Barcelona.
  • NVIDIA 6G Innovation Platform:
    • An agent-driven testbed combining digital twin simulation, CUDA-accelerated RAN, and over-the-air connectivity via the DGX Spark and the "telco AI engineer" agentic framework (achieving a 3.6x latency improvement).

Post-Trained Models & Agentic Network Operations

  • NEMOTRON & Partner Implementations:
    • Cisco: Announced Crosswork AI powered by NVIDIA Neotron 3, demonstrating superior deep network troubleshooting, KPI evaluation, and hypothesis generation.
    • TCS & BT: Site installation photos are evaluated by Neotron on-site via agents, cutting evaluation times from 3+ weeks down to immediate verification before technicians leave the site.
    • NTT Data: Building predictive degradation analysis products for global telco operators.
    • Nokia Partnership: Post-trained Neotron across RAN, transport, and cloud domains to accelerate cross-domain root cause analysis and decision-making.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Nokia Spectral Efficiency Targets: Nokia targets a 50% spectral efficiency improvement by next year and a 100% (2x) improvement the year after (attributed by Ronnie Vasishta to Nokia).
  • BT Live Trials Timeline: BT will run Nokia software in live trials in Birmingham, UK, in 2027 (attributed by Ronnie Vasishta to BT).

Garry Tan: AI Will Replace Jobs, Create New Billionaires, and Change Everything

The Builders with Walter Isaacson(22.3K)·36:45·2026-11-03
Garry TanCEO, Y Combinator Walter IsaacsonHost, The Builders
TL;DRY Combinator CEO Garry Tan warns of an incoming AI oligarchy controlled by "five kings," arguing that aggressive antitrust enforcement, open-source ecosystems, and "little tech" startups are essential to counter corporate gatekeeping.
The 10-Minute Interview Constraint: YC receives 80,000 to 90,000 applications annually for roughly 800 spots (under a 1% acceptance rate), conducting final interviews that last exactly 10 minutes. Core Criteria: Founders must concisely articulate a real user with a real...

TL;DR

Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan warns of an incoming AI oligarchy controlled by "five kings," arguing that aggressive antitrust enforcement, open-source ecosystems, and "little tech" startups are essential to counter corporate gatekeeping.

Y Combinator & Founder Vetting

  • The 10-Minute Interview Constraint: YC receives 80,000 to 90,000 applications annually for roughly 800 spots (under a 1% acceptance rate), conducting final interviews that last exactly 10 minutes.
    • Core Criteria: Founders must concisely articulate a real user with a real problem, how their technology solves it, and why they are uniquely suited to build it.
    • Alumni Impact: Roughly 10% to 15% of all self-made, sub-50-year-old billionaires are YC alumni.
  • Capital & Meritocracy:
    • Funding Scale: YC provides $500,000 per startup, a massive increase from the $18,000 provided when Tan went through the program, designed to let individuals participate without rich parents.
    • Meritocratic Aspiration: While true meritocracy is impossible, Tan argues it remains a vital virtue to pursue, combating historical access barriers based on prep school or Stanford business school networks.

Big Tech, Antitrust, & "Little Tech"

  • The Antidote to Monopoly: Tan argues that Big Tech companies are behaving like historical railroad monopolies by practicing self-preferencing and closing platform ecosystems.
    • Historical Precedent: Google was able to thrive as a trillion-dollar company largely because of the Department of Justice's consent decree on Microsoft.
    • Current Enforcement: Platforms like Apple and Google use security and privacy "smokescreens" to lock down operating systems and iMessages, killing startup access.
  • Policy & Political Engagement:
    • YC Policy Arm: YC runs a lean policy operation in D.C. led by Luther Lowe, operating on a budget roughly 1/100th the size of a single Big Tech firm to fight astroturf lobbying.
    • Regulatory Models: Tan points to the European Union's Digital Markets Act (DMA) and the Open App Markets Act as correct regulatory models for forcing data portability, reverse engineering, and open app markets.

AI Agents, Infrastructure, & the "Five Kings" Risk

  • AI Agents as Operating Systems: Tan views AI not as a mere chat feature, but as a core OS with massive working memory capacity—capable of retaining thousands of pages (equivalent to three Harry Potter books) and acting autonomously.
    • Personal Cost: Tan spends $100,000 to $200,000 a year on tokens to run hyper-customized AI agent stacks that ingest his emails, Dropbox, Apple notes, and 20 years of therapy notes.
    • Personalized Social Media: AI agents could replace monolithic social apps like Meta, allowing users to run cognitive filters that curate feeds locally based on personal goals rather than corporate engagement algorithms.
  • The "Five Kings" Threat:
    • AI labs and frontier model makers risk collapsing all future commercial value into five dominant entities.
    • Open Source Deficiency: Tan notes a major geopolitical vulnerability: there are zero open-source AI companies in the United States, giving Chinese model companies a massive advantage due to a lack of distillation-friendly American regulations.

Economic Impact & Job Displacement

  • Productivity Multipliers: Using tools like Claude Code and his open-source project G-stack, developers and founders can achieve 400 times the output.
  • Retraining & Human Agency:
    • The primary responsibility of CEOs is not easy cost-cutting, which risks a dystopian accumulation of capital.
    • Displaced workers (such as medical billers) must be paired with young technologists or use AI agency to build new companies based on their unique domain expertise.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Personal AI Adoption: "It seems inevitable in about two to three years, I would say, everyone's going to do it, and it's going to feel awesome." — Garry Tan
  • Browser Wars Redux: "Next year will be one of the defining years where there's the next browser war... And I think it'll go similar to operating systems. Like I think there will probably be a Mac, there will probably be a PC." — Garry Tan

Notable Quotes

"If you say I'm staying at the Motel 6, that says something about what Palantir is... And guess what, Palantir is not Motel 6 software. Palantir is Mandarin Oriental software." — *Alex Karp (recounted by Garry Tan)*
"Most tech people are not interested in politics, but politics sure is interested in us." — *Garry Tan*
"We live in a world where there aren't startups. That's like, a bit of a nightmare scenario for me. Like, I think that this is the counterweight against tyranny and frankly mismanagement in Big Tech." — *Garry Tan*

We will never be, you know, I think that the woke age of sort of like five or 10 years ago there we sort of lost sight of that, that like meritocracy could never be achieved, but it is a virtue to attempt to achieve it. Nowadays, it's very hard to sort of get a steady job, and the economy for young people tends to depend more and more on startups. I mean, that's why YC gives $500,000, for instance. When I did YC, it was only $18,000. Like, today you hear about seed funds and Stanford kids raising 30 million on 300 million post-money valuation. It's like literally like it's just an idea and one person, and like the company is somehow worth 300 million, but when I was, when we were starting many years ago, it was a very small amount of money. You had to show that you had something that was great. And so, you know, that's why YC didn't stay and you know, we we easily could have just stayed at 12 or $15,000, but I think there was a recognition that, if we want to be a thing that allows people to participate, you don't have to have rich parents. Like, you know, the half a million dollars getting into YC, like you can just have an idea, and you can come in and meet us for 10 minutes. You were one of the earliest employees at Palantir. Inside fal’s $1.65B Series E & F Led by Sequoia & Bessemer

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(78.1K)·63:37·3.8K views·2026-11-03
Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery Burkay GürCo-Founder and CEO, fal Gorkem YurtsevenCo-Founder and CTO, fal
TL;DRGenerative media is one of only three true product-market fits in AI alongside search and coding agents, prompting fal to build the infrastructure powering image, video, audio, and 3D models for developers and studios.
Co-founders Burkay Gür and Gorkem Yurtseven explain how the company evolved from Python data infrastructure into a generative media powerhouse driving a 150-person organization with just two reporting levels. Founding Thesis: Burkay Gür and Gorkem Yurtseven started fal in 2021...

TL;DR

Generative media is one of only three true product-market fits in AI alongside search and coding agents, prompting fal to build the infrastructure powering image, video, audio, and 3D models for developers and studios. Co-founders Burkay Gür and Gorkem Yurtseven explain how the company evolved from Python data infrastructure into a generative media powerhouse driving a 150-person organization with just two reporting levels.

Company Origin and Pivot to Generative Media

  • Founding Thesis: Burkay Gür and Gorkem Yurtseven started fal in 2021 with the thesis that Python would be the next big data infrastructure wave—mirroring what Snowflake and Databricks did for SQL by owning the cloud compute layer.
  • The Pivot to AI: After building cloud infrastructure for machine learning workloads, the breakthrough of chat models and early image models (Stable Diffusion, DALL-E) prompted a strategic shift away from the pre-AI data infrastructure business, which had generated zero revenue in its first year.
  • Naming Generative Media: Recognizing that image and video models solved fundamentally different problems than LLMs (targeting ads, marketing, and studios), the founders coined the term "generative media" and put it on their website three years ago when no one else understood the category.

Business Growth and Revenue Dynamics

  • Current Revenue Split: Video and image models now split fal's revenue 50/50, surprising the founders who initially believed video would completely cannibalize image models.
  • Resurgence of Image Models: Image editing models brought image models back to life because they allow users to create precise starting frames that video models can then effectively interpolate between.
  • Infinite Demand Despite Rising Costs: When video models like V2 jumped in price from roughly 30–40 cents per 5-second clip to $5 per 5-second clip, the team initially doubted who would pay; instead, usage surged as professional use cases in ads and movie production expanded.
  • Compute Constraints: The company acts as an intimate infrastructure and API partner for model labs and application builders during a severe compute shortage, anticipating growth to procure compute and enable ecosystem launches.

Team Culture and Flat Organizational Design

  • Team Scale and Backgrounds: fal operates with a ~150-person organization, drawing heavily on talent from Coinbase and Amazon networks built over the founders' careers.
  • Hiring Philosophy: The company recruits for intense domain interest and "hunger," echoing historical crypto-quiz hiring tactics from Coinbase to find engineers who are genuinely obsessed with the space.
  • Two-Level Hierarchy: fal maintains an exceptionally flat structure with only two reporting levels, relying on "super ICs" (individual contributors who can both execute hands-on work and lead small teams of 5 to 10 engineers) rather than pure managers.
  • Work-Life Realities: Despite the high-stakes environment, the company encourages realistic boundaries, noting that engineers go home on weekends while still achieving high velocity.

Industry Evolution and Future Modalities

  • Modality Expansion: Generative media encompasses multiple sub-categories and modalities—including text-to-image, image editing, video, 3D/4D/5D for gaming, and text/speech translation—with roughly 5 to 10 companies competing in each sub-category.
  • Abundant Training Data: Unlike code or specific LLM domains, video training data enjoys an abundance of freely available content; the hard problem is filtering out interesting physics and world data rather than running out of raw material.
  • World Models and Gaming: Emerging world models allow users to navigate persistent 3D/interactive environments via prompts, which the founders believe could unlock gaming as the next massive AI product-market fit.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Software Engineering Speed — *Gorkem Yurtseven* claims that with AI assistance, fal’s software engineering team moves 3 to 4x faster, meaning they would otherwise need 4 times the headcount to maintain the same velocity.
  • Timeline for Evergreen Content — *Burkay Gür* predicts that within the next 12 months, the industry will produce its first "timeless AI content"—a widely accepted short movie or animation that people will look back on years later and enjoy the same way they do upon release.

Notable Quotes

  • > “The consensus view was there's going to be more margin in the application layer and I think it's completely reversed. Margins are close to zero in the application layer... You can only create margin if you are in the compute path.”

PIMCO CEO Manny Roman Talks Markets, AI, Rate Outlook | Bloomberg Talks

Bloomberg Podcasts(610.0K)·14:08·564 views·2026-11-03
Emmanuel RomanCEO, PIMCO David InglesHost, Bloomberg News Yvonne ManHost, Bloomberg News
TL;DRPIMCO CEO Emmanuel Roman views current fixed income markets as highly attractive—offering 7% to 8% blended returns—while noting that elevated interest rates are driven by energy and Middle Eastern geopolitical factors rather than AI financing.
Speaking with Bloomberg's David Ingles and Yvonne Man at the Global Financial Leaders' Investment Summit in Hong Kong, Roman also discussed structural challenges in Europe, US midterm probabilities, and why long-term technological trends like AI and robotics are deflationary for...

TL;DR

PIMCO CEO Emmanuel Roman views current fixed income markets as highly attractive—offering 7% to 8% blended returns—while noting that elevated interest rates are driven by energy and Middle Eastern geopolitical factors rather than AI financing. Speaking with Bloomberg's David Ingles and Yvonne Man at the Global Financial Leaders' Investment Summit in Hong Kong, Roman also discussed structural challenges in Europe, US midterm probabilities, and why long-term technological trends like AI and robotics are deflationary for the labor market.

US Midterms and Political Risk

  • Democratic Sweep Probability: Prediction Market Odds: There is a 60% to 65% chance that Democrats win both the House and the Senate, according to prediction markets cited by Roman.
  • Policy Impact: PIMCO colleague Libby Cantrol is tracking individual races and the scale of potential House gains, which will dictate subsequent policy changes.
  • State-Level AI Fragmentation: Data Center Regulations: Views across different US states regarding data center restrictions vary widely, dictating the physical limits of compute construction alongside broader political uncertainties.

Sovereign Markets and Global Rates

  • Rate Drivers: Energy and Geopolitics: PIMCO's view is that rate levels are driven by Middle Eastern events and the energy complex, not AI debt or financing. Swap spreads and TIPS indicate the market is well-anchored, with $500 billion in AI financing causing no visible swap curve distortion.
  • Central Bank Outlook:
    • US Fed: The market and PIMCO view indicate the Fed will raise rates by a quarter-point at the end of the year and then pause.
    • Europe: The European Central Bank will likely need to raise rates twice due to a more difficult economic backdrop.
  • Global Rate Comparison: US rates sit at roughly the same level as French rates, but US rates are a much more attractive 5-year investment option considering currency and appreciation potential.

Europe and China Economic Landscapes

  • European Structural Challenges: Europe faces very low growth, high debt-to-GDP ratios, high taxation with little room to raise taxes further, and popular discontent across France, Germany, and Switzerland resulting from 25 years of globalization and industrial challenges.
  • Currency Diversification: The US Dollar's Status: While investors may hold Australian dollars or favored emerging market currencies, currency diversification outside the US dollar is not as attractive as hoped, cementing the dollar's position as the primary reserve currency.
  • China's Bond Market and Economy:
    • Bond Performance: Chinese bond yields have dropped from 3% to 4% down to 1.5%.
    • Investment Environment: PIMCO finds China's tight market offers little to do for a global bond portfolio compared to other regions, though savings remain high in Chinese banks rather than signaling a crisis.
    • The Yuan's Future: The single most important macro decision over the next 20 years is whether China will decide to let its currency float, which could turn the yuan into a second reserve currency at the cost of potential capital flight.

Hong Kong Summit and Global Fixed Income

  • Hong Kong's Recovery: Strong Event Energy: Praised the HKMA, Eddie Yue, and his team for hosting a vibrant event that exceeded expectations, highlighting that Hong Kong is operating at full speed and serving as a vital gateway to China.
  • Portfolio Strategy and Popular Allocations:
    • Blended Returns: Investors can capture 7% to 8% blended returns in fixed income with attractive risk-adjusted profiles compared to equity markets and expensive S&P valuations.
    • Capital Rebalancing: Cash in the system is going into fixed income to rebalance portfolios and take chips off the table globally (US, Europe, Asia).
  • Attractive Value Pockets: US and global bonds, mortgages, emerging markets, asset-backed securities, structured private finance (yielding 12% to 15%), and European data center builds offer compelling opportunities.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Global Reserve Currency Decision: Over the next 20 years, China's single most important macro decision will be whether to let its currency float, which would establish the yuan as a second reserve currency (Emmanuel Roman).
  • US Fed Rate Path: The Federal Reserve will raise rates by a quarter of a point at the end of the year and then stop (Emmanuel Roman).
  • European Central Bank Rate Path: The ECB will likely have to raise rates twice (Emmanuel Roman).
  • Long-Term AI Deflation: Artificial intelligence is capital-intensive from very strong hands, but it is long-term deflationary, particularly regarding its impact on the labor market alongside robotics (Emmanuel Roman).

Notable Quotes

"If you are a US investors like most people in the region and you say where else do I want to be money do I want to put money... the diversification in terms of currency is not as attractive as one would hope and so the days of a strong dollars are likely to remain." — Emmanuel Roman
"If you had told me 5 years ago that the city will be at full speed doing great, I think it's wonderful and and and I think it has exceeded a lot of people's expectation rightly or wrongly." — Emmanuel Roman
"The beauty of AI is it is capex intensive from very strong hands, but long-term deflationary." — Emmanuel Roman

Sam Altman On AI Revolution, Superintelligence & Future Of Humanity | FO583 Raj Shamani

Raj Shamani(21.5M)·68:00·2026-11-03
Sam AltmanCEO, OpenAI Raj ShamaniHost, Figuring Out
TL;DRSam Altman reflects on building OpenAI, navigating the high-stakes world of artificial intelligence, and his journey from an underestimated startup founder to leading a historic technological revolution.
Childhood Nerd Identity: Sam Altman describes himself as a nerdy kid who was not good at sports, connecting well with friends but never feeling like part of the "cool crowd," though he still had a happy childhood with his computer and a core group of friends. Non-Consensus...

TL;DR

Sam Altman reflects on building OpenAI, navigating the high-stakes world of artificial intelligence, and his journey from an underestimated startup founder to leading a historic technological revolution.

Early Life, Mindset, and the Path to OpenAI

  • Childhood Nerd Identity: Sam Altman describes himself as a nerdy kid who was not good at sports, connecting well with friends but never feeling like part of the "cool crowd," though he still had a happy childhood with his computer and a core group of friends.
  • Non-Consensus Bets: Sam Altman believes that being misunderstood by most people is a strength rather than a weakness as long as you are right, noting that starting OpenAI was initially viewed as an extremely low-status, non-serious endeavor.
  • The Power of Scale: Sam Altman identifies the surprising properties of scale as a core insight guiding his career, observing early on that neural networks improved significantly with scale long before formal scaling laws were established.

OpenAI, Scaling, and High-Stakes Decisions

  • The GPT-4 Compute Bet: Following the completion of GPT-4 in August 2022, Sam Altman and the OpenAI team made a massive, highly scrutinized bet to acquire significant compute resources, risking the company's future on their conviction in scaling laws.
  • Launching ChatGPT: Sam Altman pushed to launch ChatGPT against the initial hesitation of others who thought it was too early and had poor retention numbers, applying the YC lesson that a small group of users who deeply love a product matters more than broad lukewarm reception.
  • India's Massive Adoption: Sam Altman notes that India is OpenAI's biggest market by touch users and is growing very rapidly, driven by developing economic conditions and high builder energy.
  • Avoiding Yes-Men: To prevent being surrounded by yes-men as his power and success grew, Sam Altman relies on old friends of 10+ years and actively cultivates a culture that rewards hard pushback when he is wrong.

Startup Insights, Ambition, and Mentorship

  • Training Ambition: Sam Altman discovered at Y Combinator that ambition can indeed be taught to founders by placing them in strong peer groups and guiding them through successive challenges of increasing difficulty.
  • The Meta-Advice on Advice: Sam Altman advises founders to listen to less advice, emphasizing that beyond foundational axiomatic truths (like making something people want), endless podcast advice is unhelpful compared to doing the actual work.
  • The Compounding Curve: Sam Altman warns that many people fail to stay on the compounding exponential career curve, noting that true career growth requires continuously tackling uncomfortable, non-obvious new things rather than repeating the same year of experience.

Personal Toll, Fears, and the Future of AI

  • Lifestyle Changes: Running OpenAI transformed Sam Altman from a carefree person with spontaneous free time and many hobbies into a precisely scheduled individual who can no longer enjoy simple anonymized activities like sitting in a coffee shop.
  • Pluralism vs. Power Concentration: Sam Altman expresses a deep dislike for extreme power concentration, believing it is much healthier for the world to have multiple competing AI efforts and approaches rather than a single dominant entity.
  • Scientific Discovery as the Next Frontier: Sam Altman is extremely excited about pushing AI to discover new science, solve deep insights in physics, and accelerate medical treatments and new energy sources.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Sam Altman predicts that machines will soon be pushed to discover new science, stating that recent math results make him believe humanity is closer to this milestone than previously expected.

Notable Quotes

"If you're not a little embarrassed of your first version, you're waiting too long to launch because what you need is feedback." — Sam Altman
