The Race To Privatize Space — Without Killing Everyone | Compound Interest

Semafor(35.3K)·48:40·411 views·2026-11-03
Dylan TaylorCEO, Voyager Technologies
TL;DRSpace is rapidly transitioning from a government-led domain to a private-sector gold rush driven by reusable rockets, with leaders like Voyager Technologies building infrastructure for national security, lunar missions, and commercial space stations.
While the industry faces technical and orbital debris hurdles, proponents argue that space-based infrastructure and data centers will soon become foundational to the global economy. - Income via Asset Control: Voyager CEO Dylan Taylor argues that free cash flow is a derivative...

TL;DR

Space is rapidly transitioning from a government-led domain to a private-sector gold rush driven by reusable rockets, with leaders like Voyager Technologies building infrastructure for national security, lunar missions, and commercial space stations. While the industry faces technical and orbital debris hurdles, proponents argue that space-based infrastructure and data centers will soon become foundational to the global economy.

Space as an Emerging Business and Asset Class

  • Income via Asset Control: Voyager CEO Dylan Taylor argues that free cash flow is a derivative of income, which depends on owning foundational asset bases in space infrastructure—similar to energy or healthcare on Earth.
  • The Launch Cost Revolution: The shift from disposable rockets to reusable launch systems (pioneered by SpaceX) has slashed costs to roughly $1,000 per pound to orbit, enabling a flourishing commercial market.
  • Venture Dynamics and M&A: Voyager utilizes proprietary deal-flow (such as its acquisition of lunar lander company Astrobotic) rather than participating in M&A auctions, targeting mission-driven founders over traditional corporate buyouts.
  • Capital Access: Voyager recently raised $400 million via a 0% coupon convertible note in 48 hours with $2 billion in investor demand, highlighting a bifurcation where high-quality space companies enjoy low costs of capital while weaker players struggle.

The Three "L"s of Future Space Strategy

  • Low Earth Orbit (LEO): Home to satellite constellations, Starlink, and commercial space stations like Voyager's upcoming Starlab (slated to succeed the International Space Station, which is expected to deorbit around 2030).
  • Lunar Operations: Returning to the moon to stay, requiring power solutions, habitation, and lunar landers to harvest potential resources like helium-3 and rare earth minerals—though complicated by a lack of international case law and competition with China.
  • Lagrange Points: Stable orbits about a million miles from Earth (such as the James Webb Telescope) where objects remain indefinitely due to balanced gravitational pull, offering significant national security advantages.

Data Centers, Debris, and Orbital Infrastructure

  • Data Centers in Orbit: Moving data centers to space is viewed as a viable business strategy to bypass terrestrial permitting and power constraints. However, cooling limitations dictate smaller form factors (e.g., home refrigerator-sized Starlink mesh units) rather than school bus-sized facilities.
  • The Space Debris Challenge: While smaller satellites are designed to burn up cleanly upon reentry, larger structures like the ISS require dedicated deorbit vehicles to safely splash down in the ocean.
  • Civilization-Threatening Asteroids: Taylor highlights asteroid monitoring as a critical long-term driver for getting more assets and better telemetry into orbit to detect and deflect potential impactors.

Geopolitics, National Security, and the "Golden Dome"

  • The New Space Race: The post-Cold War consensus of space as a peaceful global common ground has faded, replaced by great-power competition, particularly with China.
  • National Security Integration: Voyager operates heavily in defense, contributing to projects like the "Golden Dome," which Taylor describes as multi-layered defensive "onion layers" designed to counter space-based threats.
  • Proxies and Stability: Taylor suggests that current global stability relies on a technological standoff between the US and China, arguing that incremental, matched capabilities keep conflict risks relatively low.

Space Tourism and Consumer Horizons

  • Commercial Spaceflight Economics: Suborbital tourism (via Blue Origin and Virgin Galactic) is paving the way for orbital tourism. Taylor notes that while current orbital flights run around $60 million (such as Jared Isaacman’s Inspiration4), lower-cost future vehicles could drastically expand accessibility.
  • Point-to-Point Travel: SpaceX's Starship opens the theoretical possibility of hyper-fast terrestrial travel—such as New York to Tokyo in 25 minutes for the cost of a business class ticket.
  • The Ultimate Vision: Taylor predicts that within a decade, internet traffic will be primarily space-based, cell phones will communicate directly with satellites, and humanity will establish permanent settlements on the moon and eventually Mars.

Notable Quotes

"Income is a derivative of assets... If you're in the space infrastructure game, if you have the ability to get to orbit, manipulate things on orbit, move things around on orbit, and you control those asset bases, then you are going to generate a lot of income." — Dylan Taylor

The company building America’s uranium supply from scratch

Cheeky Pint(30.2K)·59:18·9.1K views·2026-11-03
Scott NolanCo-Founder and CEO, General Matter
TL;DRGeneral Matter, led by CEO Scott Nolan, is building a domestic uranium enrichment facility in Paducah, Kentucky, to supply High-Assay Low-Enriched Uranium (HALEU) and replace Russian imports ahead of a 2028 Congressional ban.
Historical Post-Cold War Shift: Following World War II and the Cold War (when the US produced over 90% of global supply), the US shut down its domestic enrichment plants and relied on the "Megatons to Megawatts" program, blending down imported Russian warheads. Current Import...

TL;DR

General Matter, led by CEO Scott Nolan, is building a domestic uranium enrichment facility in Paducah, Kentucky, to supply High-Assay Low-Enriched Uranium (HALEU) and replace Russian imports ahead of a 2028 Congressional ban.

Background & Geopolitics of Uranium Supply

  • Historical Post-Cold War Shift: Following World War II and the Cold War (when the US produced over 90% of global supply), the US shut down its domestic enrichment plants and relied on the "Megatons to Megawatts" program, blending down imported Russian warheads.
  • Current Import Dependency: The US currently sources about a quarter of its enriched uranium from Russia (operating under waivers until the ban takes effect), with the remainder supplied by European providers like France and the Urenco consortium.
  • The 2028 Ban: Congress passed a ban on imports of Russian uranium that goes into effect on January 1, 2028, creating an urgent domestic gap that General Matter aims to fill.

Nuclear Fuel, Reactors, and the HALEU Shift

  • The 5-Step Fuel Stack: Uranium is (1) mined/milled from the ground into U3O8, (2) converted into a gas, (3) separated/enriched to increase U-235 density, (4) turned back into a solid, and (5) manufactured into fuel pellets.
  • Traditional vs. Advanced Reactors:
    • Traditional light water reactors (such as the 94 grid reactors providing 20% of US power) use low-enriched uranium (LEU) at 3-5% enrichment and require massive containment domes.
    • Modern Small Modular Reactors (SMRs) and advanced reactors require HALEU (High-Assay Low-Enriched Uranium) enriched to just under 20% (specifically 19.75%).
  • Why HALEU Matters: Higher enrichment allows for a much smaller core size, enabling factory-built modular reactors and significantly longer refueling cycles (e.g., 5-to-10-year intervals) for remote or off-grid deployments.
  • Regulatory Oversight: Commercial nuclear operations are regulated by the NRC (Nuclear Regulatory Commission), which Nolan notes focuses efficiently on safety, security, and low environmental impact.

General Matter’s Execution Strategy & Paducah Facility

  • Founding & Team: Founded in late 2023 by Scott Nolan (after 10 years at Founders Fund), General Matter has scaled to just over 200 people with headquarters in Los Angeles.
  • Facility Location: Building a first commercial facility in Paducah, Kentucky—on the site of the last US enrichment facility (shut down in 2013)—chosen for community familiarity, existing talent, and pre-characterized DOE site environmental benefits.
  • In-House Construction: Copied the Tesla playbook by establishing an in-house engineering, procurement, and construction (EPC) general contracting team rather than outsourcing facility construction.

Nuclear Cost Structures and the 1970s Stasis

  • Root Cause of Nuclear Stasis: Nolan argues that high costs and stalled nuclear deployment were primarily driven by 1970s macroeconomic shocks (oil price spikes, mortgage interest rates hitting 18% in 1981) rather than safety panics like Three Mile Island or Chernobyl.
  • The Cost-Plus Trap: Traditional aerospace and nuclear primes operated under cost-plus models that lacked downward economic pressure to innovate, reduce prices, or execute rapidly.
  • Competitive Advantages: General Matter avoids legacy stagnation by applying decades of untapped post-1950s/60s technology improvements, clean-sheet design, and lean execution modeled after SpaceX.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Production Timeline: General Matter will produce HALEU before the end of the decade, with external milestones aligned to the January 1, 2028 Russian import ban and early-to-late commercial reactor deployments in 2028–2029 (Scott Nolan).
  • Grid and Market Growth: Advanced nuclear SMRs will grow tremendously over the next decade, though near-term demand for enrichers will be driven by existing utility fleet customers before transitioning to data center baseload power (Scott Nolan).

Notable Quotes

"For us, enrichment has been done before. A number of countries do it. But the real challenge is we don't only want to bring it back in the US, we want to make it globally competitive." — Scott Nolan
"The thing I walked away with... was these small teams. Even though they seem completely outmatched, by moving quickly with a good group of people, you can still beat incumbents." — Scott Nolan

Which pieces? The smaller core and highly enriched uranium. To unlock a smaller core design that could be built in a factory, you want your fuel to be higher octane, so to speak, higher enrichment level. The reason you care about your fuel being at a higher enrichment level is because at some point, as it's consumed, as it reacts, that enrichment level will drop. At some point, you can think of it as the power going out, the reactor fizzling out. If you have a stationary 1-gigawatt reactor, you're running 3-5% enriched uranium. You're probably doing refueling every 18 months, 24 months. That's an acceptable thing. You have a very big reactor, your core will stay critical for quite a while, and the refueling every 18, 24 months is not such a big deal. If you have a very small core, think of it as you need more higher octane fuel. As it burns up, it will pass through acceptable power production down to not even being critical more quickly. Some of these reactors also want to have 5-year, 10-year refueling cycles because they're out somewhere remote, not somewhere central. It's a combination of you want to unlock a smaller core that will remain critical for a longer period. That makes sense. Can you talk about the passive safety advantages of highly enriched uranium that you mentioned? Not highly, but HALEU. Sorry. Yeah. The enrichment level itself does not lead to safety advantages, but people's reactor formats do. Some of these reactors are passively safe. A classically well-known version of this was molten salt reactors. The molten salt has thermal properties that cause it to self-regulate, and then if somehow it were overheated, you could put a plug in the bottom of the reservoir, and then the plug would melt, and then everything drains out, and criticality stops. That's the idea of a molten salt reactor. You have other ones like helium-cooled, where the helium can't be activated, the helium cannot become radioactive. So, even if you had a cooling release, you're not releasing any radioactive material. In general, these newer designs just have either passive safety features or modern architectures around active safety. You can design into the reactor some self-correcting mechanism, like a fuse plug-type thing, where the act of starting to run away from you causes the safety mechanisms to kick in. Right. And then the way it gets solved depends on what you think the cause of the cost issues in nuclear are being. This is one of those overdetermined problems of, "Why haven't we scaled nuclear?" I think most people just say safety. They look back to the '70s, and they say: "There was a movie, The China Syndrome. You had Three Mile Island. You had Chernobyl. We weren't doing it quickly enough. We never built out the supply chains. The workforces would shift around from project to project. Vogtle was way over budget, way over schedule. That schedule philosophy of green lights to Malibu, it comes out of this idea that if you give people more time, they will simply take more time. There is a minimum... And you can maybe go too quick and squeeze all time out of the schedule to the point where now you're doing things that are not in support of the correct architecture or a very long-term cost structure. That's always the critique. That is the tension. That's the tension. That's even the internal tension at any company trying to run a green lights to Malibu schedule, is: Number 1, do you have waste? Number 2, do you make the wrong architectural decisions? Then number 3, how do you manage people against this plan? If you're doing performance-based management, and you give someone a very difficult plan that maybe is perfection, it's more difficult to judge the performance against that. You have to have in your own mind, "What's a little bit of buffer? What's a reasonable thing?" It's often very useful. The green lights to Malibu is not only a scheduling tool, it's also a constraints tool of if we really try and dial this in and remove all buffer, what breaks? What's the thing that's the critical path that's preventing us from... It's a bottleneck discovery tool. I think it can be, when done properly. You Can't Tell What's Real Anymore. Here's What Comes Next - Cristóbal Valenzuela, Runway

AI Engineer(983.0K)·16:51·10.8K views·2026-11-03
Cristóbal ValenzuelaCo-founder and CEO, Runway
TL;DRRunway co-founder and CEO Cristóbal Valenzuela explains how scaling next-frame video prediction teaches models real-world physics, enabling interactive world interface models, robotics, and early operating system prototypes like Continuum where any screen content becomes a live simulation.
Prehistoric AI Roots: Runway's first AI film in 2019, "The Orange," generated pixels at a 128x128 resolution before being forcibly upsampled. Current Generation Quality: Modern video models generate characters and locations that successfully pass the Turing test, with human...

TL;DR

Runway co-founder and CEO Cristóbal Valenzuela explains how scaling next-frame video prediction teaches models real-world physics, enabling interactive world interface models, robotics, and early operating system prototypes like Continuum where any screen content becomes a live simulation.

Evolution from Pixel Generation to State-of-the-Art Video

  • Prehistoric AI Roots: Runway's first AI film in 2019, "The Orange," generated pixels at a 128x128 resolution before being forcibly upsampled.
  • Current Generation Quality: Modern video models generate characters and locations that successfully pass the Turing test, with human evaluators unable to distinguish generated footage from real video.
  • Art and Science Intersection: The company maintains deep roots in artistic and creative communities alongside a research lab of top scientists specializing in diffusion models and large video models.

Physics and Simulation via Next-Frame Prediction

  • Cinema as Optical Illusion: Video functions as an optical illusion created by stitching frames fast enough to produce motion pictures.
  • Emergent World Physics: Scaling next-frame prediction causes models to inherently learn complex real-world mechanics without explicit programming:
    • Core Physical Laws: Models learn gravity, occlusion, and movement.
    • Object Interactions: Models simulate how objects interact, such as a camel standing on a ball or water moving inside a container.
  • Simulation Systems without Rules: By scaling next-frame prediction in 3D environments, models function like physics engines and game systems—such as generating non-GTA interactive virtual worlds—without explicit descriptions of physical laws.

Real-Time Interaction and World Interface Models

  • From Async to Real-Time: While traditional generation takes seconds or minutes, the next 3 to 4 years of video models focus on real-time interactivity where users can act at any point during frame prediction.
  • Robotics Integration: Runway has spun up an entire robotics lab in its New York office to use these video models as the brains for autonomous, embodied systems.
  • World Interface Models: Images can be transformed into interactive applications (e.g., turning on car lights, opening a hood, or moving objects in a room) without any underlying 3D representation like Unity, Unreal, or Blender.

Continuum: Video Models as Operating Systems

  • Early Research Prototype: Continuum integrates video models directly into daily computing, streaming operating system content straight from a video model.
  • Portals and Memory: Users can select any text on a website and instantly spawn an interactive real-time video "portal" based on the content, while a desktop memory tracks past visualizations.
  • Reactive Spatial Scaling: Resizing an application window dynamically changes the behavior and visual experience—such as navigating cellular structures during a lesson on photosynthesis.
  • Visual Thinking for Coding Agents: Connecting Continuum to a coding agent allows the agent to visually think through problems (like generating a 3D model of a bicycle) rather than relying solely on code.
  • Live Reactive Experiences: Searching for topics like Mars prompts the system to generate a real-time, interactive Mars Outpost interface based on actual and projected data.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Product Release Timeline: Runway will release most of the Continuum research and simulation capabilities for public use within the next 8 to 12 months — Cristóbal Valenzuela.

Notable Quotes

"Video is an optical illusion. Video is the illusion of movement, that if you do if you stitch frames fast enough, you will create movement." — Cristóbal Valenzuela
"For it to understand how to make this a consistent video [a complex scene], [the model] will need to naturally understand what all these objects are, how each of them relate to one another... and you're doing that all through just video, through images, through through next frame prediction." — Cristóbal Valenzuela
"There's no unity, there's no unreal, there's no Blender. It's just a video model. A video model that has been trained to understand actions and clicks in an interface." — Cristóbal Valenzuela
